Regularisation by multiplicative noise for reaction–diffusion equations
Authors: Konstantinos Dareiotis, Teodor Holland, Khoa Lê
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We consider the stochastic reaction–diffusion equation in 1+11+1 dimensions driven by multiplicative space–time white noise, with a distributional drift belonging to a Besov–Hölder space with any regularity index strictly larger than -1-1. We assume that the diffusion coefficient is a regular function which is bounded away from zero. By using a combination of stochastic sewing techniques and Malliavin calculus, we show that the equation admits a unique solution.

Introduction

This paper is concerned with regularisation by noise phenomena for the stochastic reaction–diffusion equation on the 1-dimensional torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T} := \mathbb {R}/\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}T:=R/Z driven by multiplicative space–time white noise 1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _t - \Delta )u = b(u) + \sigma (u)\xi , \qquad u|_{t=0}=u_0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂t-Δ)u=b(u)+σ(u)ξ,u|t=0=u0.We show existence and uniqueness of solutions and we derive stability estimates, provided that σ σ is regular, bounded away from zero and that b is a distribution with a regularity index strictly larger than -1-1 in the Besov–Hölder scale.

For results on regularisation by noise phenomena for stochastic differential equations (SDEs), we refer the reader to [8, 17, 27, 34, 36]. Concerning stochastic partial differential equations (SPDEs), the first results on regularisation by noise can be traced back to the works of Gyöngy and Pardoux [21, 25]. Therein, the authors consider SPDEs of the form1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _t - \Delta )u = b(u) + \xi , \qquad u|_{t=0}=u_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂t-Δ)u=b(u)+ξ,u|t=0=u0,which corresponds to (1.1) with σ =1σ=1. It is well known that the deterministic counterpart of (1.2) admits a unique solution provided that b is a Lipschitz continuous function. Without Lipschitz regularity, solutions may not exist or may not be identified uniquely. The situation changes in the presence of noise. It is shown in [21] and [25] that (1.2) admits a unique strong solution provided that b is merely the sum of a bounded measurable function and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p$$\end{document}Lp-integrable function with some p \ge 2p≥2. Similar results were obtained for SPDEs in an abstract Hilbert-space framework with bounded and measurable drift in [11]. In [7], Butkovsky and Mytnik show when bb is bounded and measurable, path-by-path uniqueness also holds for (1.2). For such drift, discrete approximation schemes for the solution of (1.2) have been established with an optimal rate in [6], quantifying earlier results from [23, 24].

Notice that in all the previous results, b is quite irregular, nevertheless it is a function. The first well-posedness result which accommodates distributional drift b is due to Athreya, Butkovsky, Mytnik, and the third co-author in [2]. In such case, the composition b(u) is not well-defined a priori and solutions to (1.2) are defined in a regularised sense which is similar to that of Bass and Chen in [5] (see Definition 3.2). They show in [2] that (1.2) admits a unique probabilistically strong solution provided that b belongs to the Besov space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{q,\infty }^\alpha $$\end{document}Bq,∞α for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in [1,\infty ]$$\end{document}q∈[1,∞], with1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha - 1/q \ge -1, \qquad \alpha >-1. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}α-1/q≥-1,α>-1.Such Besov spaces include bounded measurable functions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1$$\end{document}L1-integrable functions, as well as Radon measures. To obtain such results, [2] establishes Lipschitz regularity for some related singular integrals using the stochastic sewing lemma introduced by the third co-author in [28]. The regularity threshold -1-1 is in agreement with the finite dimensional analogue [8] where it is shown that any SDE driven by additive fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter H ∈ (0,1)H∈(0,1) has a unique solution provided that the drift belongs to the Besov–Hölder space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{\infty ,\infty }^\alpha $$\end{document}B∞,∞α with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 1 - \frac{1}{2H}$$\end{document}α>1-12H. The two results are related by setting H = 1/4H=1/4, which is the temporal regularity of the random field solution of (1.2) with b =0b=0. Quantitative convergence of discrete approximation schemes under the assumptions of [2] is also considered by Goudenége, Haress and Richard in [20], extending [6].

All of the aforementioned results concern the additive noise case. For the multiplicative case, much less is known. In [1, 3, 22], the authors show that (1.1) has a unique solution when σ σ is regular and bounded away from 00, the drift is measurable and bounded/locally bounded/integrable respectively. The proofs from these references rely on the Girsanov theorem, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p$$\end{document}Lp-estimates for the density of the driftless equation (as obtained in [32]), and a comparison principle. Our result herein is an analogue of [2] for the multiplicative noise case. Namely, we show existence and uniqueness for (1.1) when σ σ is regular and bounded away from 00, the drift belongs to the Besov–Hölder space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{\infty ,\infty }^\alpha $$\end{document}B∞,∞α with α >-1α>-1. In relation to the assumptions in [2]: notice that by Sobolev’s embedding theorem, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {B}_{q,\infty }^\alpha \subset \mathscr {B}_{\infty , \infty }^{\alpha -1/q}$$\end{document}Bq,∞α⊂B∞,∞α-1/q. Consequently, we also cover the cases \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b \in \mathscr {B}_{q,\infty }^\alpha $$\end{document}b∈Bq,∞α with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q \in [1, \infty ]$$\end{document}q∈[1,∞] and α -1/q>-1α-1/q>-1. Hence, the only case that we do not cover from [2] is the case α -1/q =-1α-1/q=-1 with α >-1α>-1 (see (1.3) above). In this sense, our assumption is slightly stronger than the one from [2], but at the same time our conclusion is also stronger: not only we show well-posedness but in fact we obtain Lipschitz stability of the solution map with respect to b. This lays the groundwork for a follow-up study on the numerical approximation of the equation, where schemes based on smoothed approximations of the drift will be employed. Such estimates are not expected to be true in the borderline case α -1/q =-1α-1/q=-1. In fact, the result in [2] is not quantitative but relies on an argument of Yamada-Watanabe kind. Concerning purely the well-posedness of the equation, one could combine the critical sewing methods from [2] with the techniques developed in the present article to tackle the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha -1/q =-1, \alpha >-1$$\end{document}α-1/q=-1,α>-1 too. We have chosen not to pursue this route, as it would likely overwhelm the reader. Instead, we have opted to focus on the difficulties arising from the multiplicative noise and to highlight the essential elements of our approach. Similar to [2], our method also relies on the stochastic sewing lemma from [28] which does not rely on Girsanov theorem nor comparison principles. Therefore, the techniques within could also be applied to equations driven by Lévy noise and to systems of equations. Compared with [2], while the probabilistic properties of the noise term in the additive case are explicitly understood, this is no longer the case for our multiplicative equation (1.1). Therefore, employing the sewing methods in the present paper is more involved than [2]. In the sewing arguments in previous works, one approximates a solution using the integral form of the corresponding equation. This works quite well in the additive noise case, [2, 8]. It also works quite well in some multiplicative noise cases if the noise is not too irregular, for example equations driven by fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter H>1/2H>1/2 [14]. However, for H < 1/2H<1/2, this approach leads to suboptimal results. The same is true for the setting of the present paper. With such an approach, one would only be able to obtain well-posedness when b has positive Hölder regularity. Instead, in order to cover the whole regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b \in \mathscr {B}^\alpha _{\infty , \infty }$$\end{document}b∈B∞,∞α with α >-1α>-1, we come up with sewing arguments that employ the flow of the driftless equation (see Section 3.2 for a more detailed overview of our method). Consequently, we need (and obtain) some regularisation estimates related to the density of the solution to the driftless equation and its derivatives. These estimates are achieved via Malliavin calculus which demands a relatively high regularity from σ σ. This approach is not equation-specific but rather works as a general principle. In fact, we are using it in a parallel work ([16]) to improve the results from [14] to optimal, in the case of rough equations driven by fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter H ∈ (1/3, 1/2)H∈(1/3,1/2) .

The paper is organised as follows: In Section 2, we introduce some necessary notation. In Section 3, we define the key concepts, state the main result, and give an overview of the proof. Facts on stochastic sewing and Malliavin calculus are summarised in Section 4. In Section 5, we study the regularity of the flow of the driftless equation, provide bounds on the moments of its Malliavin derivatives, and establish relevant nondegeneracy estimates. In Section 6, we quantify how well the flow of the driftless equation approximates the solution of (1.1). In Section 7, we use the tools developed up until then to derive regularisation results for the solution of (1.1) via the stochastic sewing lemma. These estimates are then used in Section 8 to prove a stability result, and in Section 9 to estimate the drift term of (1.1). The main result is proven in Section 10. Finally, the appendix contains some useful auxiliary elementary estimates which are used throughout the article.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A\subseteq \mathbb {R}^d$$\end{document}A⊆Rd and (B,|· |)(B,|·|) be a normed space. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} continuous functions f:A → Bf:A→B such that for all multi-indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l \in (\mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0})^d$$\end{document}l∈(Z≥0)d with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \begin{document}$$|l| \le \alpha $$\end{document}|l|≤α the derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Remark 2.1

For all \varepsilon >0ε>0 we have the inclusions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\alpha + \varepsilon } \subset C^{\alpha +} \subset C^{\alpha }$$\end{document}Cα+ε⊂Cα+⊂Cα.

Formulation and main result

Formulation and main result

We introduce our main assumptions and the concept of solutions to (1.1).

Assumption 3.1

The function b is of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\alpha +}$$\end{document}Cα+ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (-1,0)$$\end{document}α∈(-1,0) and the function σ σ is of class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^4$$\end{document}C4. Moreover, there exists a positive constant μ μ such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma (x) \ge \mu \quad \text {for all }x \in {\mathbb {R}}.$$\end{document}σ(x)≥μfor allx∈R.Finally, the initial condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0 : \mathbb {T} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u0:T→R is a bounded and continuous deterministic function.

Due to continuity, the above inequality is equivalent to the usual uniform ellipticity condition that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2 (x) \ge \mu ^2$$\end{document}σ2(x)≥μ2 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X\in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}X∈R.

Definition 3.2

(Regularised solution) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: \Omega \times [0,1]\times \mathbb {T} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}u:Ω×[0,1]×T→R be a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {P}\otimes \mathscr {B}(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}P⊗B(T)-measurable random field, such that u(t, x) is continuous in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,1]×T. We say that u is a regularised solution of (1.1) if there exists a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {P}\otimes \mathscr {B}(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}P⊗B(T)-measurable random field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D^{u}:\Omega \times [0,1]\times \mathbb {T} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Du:Ω×[0,1]×T→R such that For any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b^n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}} \subset C^\infty $$\end{document}(bn)n∈N⊂C∞ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} b$$\end{document}bn→b in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\alpha }$$\end{document}Cα, we have that 3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} For any sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(b^n)_{n\in \mathbb {N}} \subset C^\infty $$\end{document}(bn)n∈N⊂C∞ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^n \rightarrow b$$\end{document}bn→b in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^{\alpha }$$\end{document}Cα, we have that 3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{(t,x) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}}\Big |D_t^u(x) -\int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}}p_{t-r}(x,y)b^n(u(r,y))dydr\Big | \longrightarrow 0\end{aligned}$$\end{document}sup(t,x)∈[0,1]×T|Dtu(x)-∫0t∫Tpt-r(x,y)bn(u(r,y))dydr|⟶0 in probability.

For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,1]×T, 3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(t,x) = P_tu_0(x) + D_t^u(x) + \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y) \sigma (u(r,y)) \xi (dy,dr)\qquad \text {a.s.} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,x)=Ptu0(x)+Dtu(x)+∫0t∫Tpt-r(x,y)σ(u(r,y))ξ(dy,dr)a.s.

Remark 3.3

For a given regularised solution u, the random field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D^u$$\end{document}Du is uniquely characterised by relation (3.1). Furthermore, in the more regular setting when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ge 0$$\end{document}α≥0, Definition 3.2 reduces to the standard notion of a mild solution. In such case, one has \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D^u_t(x)=\int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}}p_{t-r}(x,y)b(u(r,y))dydr$$\end{document}Dtu(x)=∫0t∫Tpt-r(x,y)b(u(r,y))dydr.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S,T) \in [0,1]^2$$\end{document}(S,T)∈[0,1]2 such that S łe TS≤T, let us define the simplices\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\,[S,T]_{\le }^2 := \{ (s,t) \in [S,T]^2: s \le t\}\, \text { and } \,[S,T]_{<}^2 := \{(s,t) \in [S,T]^2 : s<t\}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[S,T]≤2:={(s,t)∈[S,T]2:s≤t}and[S,T]<2:={(s,t)∈[S,T]2:s<t}.To describe the regularity of the solutions, we introduce the following spaces of random fields.

Definition 3.4

(The spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {V}_p^\beta $$\end{document}Vpβ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}_p^\beta $$\end{document}Upβand \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}^\beta $$\end{document}Uβ) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \beta \in [0, 1]$$\end{document}β∈[0,1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [1,\infty )$$\end{document}p∈[1,∞). We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {V}_p^{\beta }[0,1]$$\end{document}Vpβ[0,1] the collection of all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {P}\otimes \mathscr {B}(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}P⊗B(T)-measurable functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\Omega \times [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:Ω×[0,1]×T→R such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \mathbb {B}([0,1]\times \mathbb {T}, L_p)$$\end{document}f∈B([0,1]×T,Lp) and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${[}f]_{\mathscr {V}_p^{\beta }[0,1]} := \sup _{x \in \mathbb {T}}\sup _{(s,t) \in [0,1]_{<}^2}\frac{\Vert f_t(x) - P_{t-s}f_s(x)\Vert _{L_{p,\infty }^{\mathscr {F}_s}}}{|t-s|^{\beta }} < \infty . $$\end{document}[f]Vpβ[0,1]:=supx∈Tsup(s,t)∈[0,1]<2‖ft(x)-Pt-sfs(x)‖Lp,∞s|t-s|β<∞.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S,T) \in [0,1]^2_{\le }$$\end{document}(S,T)∈[0,1]≤2, the space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {V}_p^{\beta }[S,T]$$\end{document}Vpβ[S,T] and the corresponding seminorm are defined analogously. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}_p^\beta $$\end{document}Upβ the collection of all regularised solutions u of (1.1) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D^u \in \mathscr {V}_p^{\beta }[0,1]$$\end{document}Du∈Vpβ[0,1]. We moreover define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}^\beta := \bigcap _{p=1}^\infty \mathscr {U}_p^\beta .$$\end{document}Uβ:=⋂p=1∞Upβ.

We are now in position to state our main theorem.

Theorem 3.5

(Well-posedness) Let Assumption 3.1 hold. There exists a regularised solution u to (1.1) in the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}^{1 + \alpha /4}$$\end{document}U1+α/4. Moreover if v is another solution of (1.1) in the class \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {U}_2^\beta $$\end{document}U2β for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \ge \frac{1}{2}- \frac{\alpha }{4}$$\end{document}β≥12-α4, then u(t,x) = v(t,x)u(t,x)=v(t,x) almost surely for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,1]×T.

Overview of methods of proofs

The bulk of the proofs relies on moment estimates for singular integrals which are typically of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I:=\int _0^1\int _{\mathbb {T}}h(y)f(u(r,y))dydr \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I:=∫01∫Th(y)f(u(r,y))dydrwhere h is an integrable function, u is a solution to (1.1) and f is a distribution with negative Hölder regularity. An effective tool to estimate moments of I, which emerges from [28], is the stochastic sewing lemma. Heuristically, the lemma decomposes I corresponding to partitions of the time interval [0, 1] with vanishing mesh size. More precisely, let π π be a partition of [0, 1], then one writes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I=\sum _{[s,t]\in \pi }\int _s^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}h(y)f(u(r,y))dydr. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I=∑[s,t]∈π∫st∫Th(y)f(u(r,y))dydr.On each subinterval [s, t], we approximate the random variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _s^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}(\ldots )dydr$$\end{document}∫st∫T(…)dydr by its conditional expectation given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_s$$\end{document}Fs, i.e. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}^s\int _s^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}(\ldots )dydr$$\end{document}Es∫st∫T(…)dydr. Because the conditional law of u(r, y) given \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_s$$\end{document}Fs is unknown a priori, we further approximate u(r, y) by a random variable, denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^s(r,y)$$\end{document}ψs(r,y). There are two desirable properties for these approximations. First, one must recover I when the mesh size of π π vanishes, namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} I=\lim _{|\pi |\downarrow 0} \sum _{[s,t]\in \pi }\mathbb {E}^s\int _s^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}h(y)f(\psi ^s(r,y))dydr. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I=lim|π|↓0∑[s,t]∈πEs∫st∫Th(y)f(ψs(r,y))dydr.Second, the conditional expectation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}^s f(\psi ^s(r,y))$$\end{document}Esf(ψs(r,y)) is well-defined and can be estimated so that for some p\ge 2p≥2 and \varepsilon >0ε>0, one has3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathbb {E}^s\int _s^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}h(y)f(\psi ^s(r,y))dydr\Vert _{L_p(\Omega )}\lesssim (t-s)^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Es∫st∫Th(y)f(ψs(r,y))dydr‖Lp(Ω)≲(t-s)12+εand3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathbb {E}^s\int _a^t\int _{\mathbb {T}}h(y)[f(\psi ^s(r,y))-f(\psi ^a(r,y)]dydr\Vert _{L_p(\Omega )}\lesssim (t-s)^{1+\varepsilon } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Es∫at∫Th(y)[f(ψs(r,y))-f(ψa(r,y)]dydr‖Lp(Ω)≲(t-s)1+εfor every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\le a\le t$$\end{document}s≤a≤t. Under these two properties, the stochastic sewing lemma can be applied, and it provides estimates for the p-th moment of I.

Let us explain how \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^s$$\end{document}ψs is chosen. Relation (3.2) provides a natural decomposition of a solution as the sum of a nondegenerate noise and the drift, namely\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(t,x)&=P_tu_0(x)+D^u_t(x)+V_t(x), \text { where }\\ V_t(x)&=\int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y) \sigma (u(r,y)) \xi (dy,dr). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,x)=Ptu0(x)+Dtu(x)+Vt(x),whereVt(x)=∫0t∫Tpt-r(x,y)σ(u(r,y))ξ(dy,dr).It follows that for each słe ts≤t,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(t,x)=P_{t-s}u(s,\cdot )(x)+ [D^u_t(x)-P_{t-s}D^u_s(x)]+[V_t(x)-P_{t-s}V_s(x)]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,x)=Pt-su(s,·)(x)+[Dtu(x)-Pt-sDsu(x)]+[Vt(x)-Pt-sVs(x)].One could then choose to approximate u(t, x) by the random variable\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^s(t,x)&:=P_{t-s}u(s,\cdot )(x)+[V_t(x)-P_{t-s}V_s(x)]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψs(t,x):=Pt-su(s,·)(x)+[Vt(x)-Pt-sVs(x)].The error of this approximation can be quantified by the following estimate3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u(t,x)- \psi ^s(t,x)\Vert _{L_p(\Omega )} \lesssim |t-s|^\gamma \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u(t,x)-ψs(t,x)‖Lp(Ω)≲|t-s|γfor every słe ts≤t and for some γ >0γ>0. The larger the value of γ γ is, the better the approximation is. We note that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [V_t(x)-P_{t-s}V_s(x)] =\int _s^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y) \sigma (u(r,y)) \xi (dy,dr). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Vt(x)-Pt-sVs(x)]=∫st∫Tpt-r(x,y)σ(u(r,y))ξ(dy,dr).In the additive case (i.e. when σ σ is a constant), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_t(x)-P_{t-s}V_s(x)$$\end{document}Vt(x)-Pt-sVs(x) has a normal distribution and hence, the conditional expectation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}^s f(\psi ^s(t,x))$$\end{document}Esf(ψs(t,x)) can be evaluated precisely. The stochastic sewing method described above can be applied (i.e. achieving (3.3) and (3.4)) under some suitable regularity assumptions on f and that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma >1/2-\alpha /4 \approx 3/4$$\end{document}γ>1/2-α/4≈3/4 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \approx -1$$\end{document}α≈-1 (which is the worst regularity index imposed on the drift).

This is the approach from [2].

Going toward the multiplicative noise case, one might hope that a similar argument would work. Notice that in this case, the distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_t(x)-P_{t-s}V_s(x)$$\end{document}Vt(x)-Pt-sVs(x) conditionally on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_s$$\end{document}Fs is not known a priori. A naive way to circumvent this issue is to consider\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^s(t,x)&:=P_{t-s}u(s,\cdot )(x)+\int _s^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y) \sigma (u(s,y)) \xi (dy,dr), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψs(t,x):=Pt-su(s,·)(x)+∫st∫Tpt-r(x,y)σ(u(s,y))ξ(dy,dr),which is obtained by freezing the solution in the integrand at time s. In this way, conditionally on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_s$$\end{document}Fs, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^s(t,x)$$\end{document}ψs(t,x) once again has a normal distribution, which allows for concrete analysis. However, one can not go far with this choice as it is immediate that3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u(t,x)- \psi ^s(t,x) =\,&\int _s^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y) \big ( \sigma (u(r,y))- \sigma (u(s,y)) \big )\xi (dy,dr)\nonumber \\&{+[D^u_t(x)-P_{t-s}D^u_s(x)] }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}u(t,x)-ψs(t,x)=∫st∫Tpt-r(x,y)(σ(u(r,y))-σ(u(s,y)))ξ(dy,dr)+[Dtu(x)-Pt-sDsu(x)].The moments of the stochastic integral on the right-hand side above are (expectedly) of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |t-s|^{1/2}$$\end{document}|t-s|1/2 (consisting of two contributions of the same order 1/41/4 from the stochastic integral and from the temporal regularity of the solution). The exponent 1/21/2 falls short of the required threshold 3/43/4 which is necessary in the additive case. This makes the naive approximation (3.6) unsuitable for the sewing method under Assumption 3.1.

Moving forward, to resolve these issues, we introduce the following approximation3.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^s(t,x):= \phi ^{u(s,\cdot ),s}(t,x), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψs(t,x):=ϕu(s,·),s(t,x),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{z,s}$$\end{document}ϕz,s denotes the solution to the driftless equation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\partial _t - \Delta )\phi ^{z,s} = \sigma (\phi ^{z,s})\xi , \quad \phi ^{z,s}(s,\cdot )=z(\cdot ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(∂t-Δ)ϕz,s=σ(ϕz,s)ξ,ϕz,s(s,·)=z(·).Observe that when σ σ is a constant, (3.6) and (3.7) coincide, but otherwise they are generally different. Indeed, we show in Section 6 that the approximation (3.7) satisfies the estimate (3.5) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 1+\alpha /4$$\end{document}γ=1+α/4 which is larger than 1/2-α /41/2-α/4 as is required for the application of the sewing method. The distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^s(t,x)$$\end{document}ψs(t,x) conditioned on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_s$$\end{document}Fs might not be as explicit as in the additive noise case but nevertheless, one can extract the information which is sufficient to verify (3.3) and (3.4). This essentially boils down to obtaining estimates related to the density of the solution of the driftless equation and its derivatives, which are achieved by tools from Malliavin calculus (see Section 5.3).

When comparing our method to the existing ones from the literature, we can draw some similarities as well as genuine differences. The works [1, 3, 22] also utilise estimates on the density of the solution to the driftless equation, however, in a completely different way. In fact, these works use Girsanov theorem to extract relevant and useful a priori estimates for the solution to (1.1) from the solution of the driftless equation. Under our main assumption, the Girsanov theorem is not applicable which makes this argument obsolete. Additionally, our uniqueness argument relies on qualitative stability estimates, as opposed to comparison principles in the aforementioned works. Compare to [2], we also use stochastic sewing method. However, while [2] relies on the approximation (3.6), we introduce and utilise the better approximation (3.7). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first time it has been used in the study of regularisation by noise phenomena by sewing methods. Furthermore, because the conditional law of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^s$$\end{document}ψs is not explicit, additional works have been carried out in order to apply the sewing method successfully.

The driftless equation also appears in [9] in the study of regularisation by multiplicative fractional noise for SDEs. In this work, the authors employ a transformation, which is based on the inverse of the flow generated by the driftless equation, to transform the original equation into an additive one. Comparing the results of [9] and [14] reveals that such transformation is quite demanding and does not lead to results which are in alignment with [8]. The connection between (1.1) and the driftless equation is well-known, perhaps since the Girsanov theorem. Another instance of such relation appears in [26] in a different context. Our work therefore exhibits a new connection between the two equations.

Preliminaries

We state and recall some facts about stochastic sewing and Malliavin calculus. When a result is known, we refer to the cited references for proofs. Other statements are relatively straightforward and short proofs are given.

Stochastic sewing

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S,T) \in [0,1]_{<}^2$$\end{document}(S,T)∈[0,1]<2. For any functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}:[S,T] \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}A:[S,T]→R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:[S,T]^2_\le \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}A:[S,T]≤2→R, for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,t) \in [0,1]_{\le }^2$$\end{document}(s,t)∈[0,1]≤2 and a ∈ [s,t]a∈[s,t], we define \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_{s,t} := \mathscr {A}_t - \mathscr {A}_s$$\end{document}As,t:=At-As, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta A_{s,a,t} := A_{s,t} - A_{s,a} - A_{a,t}$$\end{document}δAs,a,t:=As,t-As,a-Aa,t.

The first stochastic sewing lemma is introduced by the third co-author in [28]. To best suit our purpose herein, we state a conditional version of the lemma which applies in settings with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{q,p}^{\mathscr {F}_s}$$\end{document}Lq,ps-norms (defined in (2.1)). This version is originated from the works [2, 19, 29], where the reader can find its proof.

Lemma 4.1

(Conditional stochastic sewing lemma) Let p, q satisfy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \le q \le p \le \infty $$\end{document}2≤q≤p≤∞ with q <∞ q<∞. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S,T) \in [0,1]_{\le }^2$$\end{document}(S,T)∈[0,1]≤2 and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:[S,T]_{\le }^2 \rightarrow L_p(\Omega )$$\end{document}A:[S,T]≤2→Lp(Ω) be a function such that for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,t) \in [S,T]_{\le }^2$$\end{document}(s,t)∈[S,T]≤2 the random vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_{s,t}$$\end{document}As,t is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_t$$\end{document}Ft-measurable. Suppose that for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1, \varepsilon _2>0$$\end{document}ε1,ε2>0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1,C_2$$\end{document}C1,C2 the bounds4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert A_{s,t}\Vert _{L_{q,p}^{\mathscr {F}_s}} \le C_1|t-s|^{1/2+\varepsilon _1}, \quad \Vert \mathbb {E}^s \delta A_{s,a,t}\Vert _{L_p} \le C_2|t-s|^{1+\varepsilon _2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖As,t‖Lq,ps≤C1|t-s|1/2+ε1,‖EsδAs,a,t‖Lp≤C2|t-s|1+ε2hold for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \le s \le a \le t \le T$$\end{document}S≤s≤a≤t≤T. Then, there exists a unique map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}:[S,T] \rightarrow L_p(\Omega )$$\end{document}A:[S,T]→Lp(Ω) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_S =0$$\end{document}AS=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}_t$$\end{document}At is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {F}_t$$\end{document}Ft-measurable for all t ∈ [S,T]t∈[S,T], and the following bounds hold for some constants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K_1,K_2>0$$\end{document}K1,K2>0:4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathscr {A}_{s,t} - A_{s,t}\Vert _{L_{q,p}^{\mathscr {F}_s}}&\le K_1 |t-s|^{1/2 + \varepsilon _1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖As,t-As,t‖Lq,ps≤K1|t-s|1/2+ε14.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathbb {E}^s(\mathscr {A}_{s,t} - A_{s,t})\Vert _{L_p}&\le K_2 |t-s|^{1+\varepsilon _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Es(As,t-As,t)‖Lp≤K2|t-s|1+ε2.Furthermore, there exists a constant K depending only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon _1,\varepsilon _2, d,p$$\end{document}ε1,ε2,d,p such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}$$\end{document}A satisfies the bound4.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathscr {A}_{s,t}\Vert _{L_{q,p}^{\mathscr {G}_s}} \le K C_1 |t-s|^{1/2 + \varepsilon _1} + K C_2 |t-s|^{1+\varepsilon _2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖As,t‖Lq,ps≤KC1|t-s|1/2+ε1+KC2|t-s|1+ε2for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(s,t)\in [S,T]_{\le }^2$$\end{document}(s,t)∈[S,T]≤2.

We will call A a germ of the process \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {A}$$\end{document}A. In practice, we mostly take q=pq=p (in which case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{q,p}^{\mathscr {F}_s}$$\end{document}Lq,ps-norm and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{p}$$\end{document}Lp-norm coincide) and p=∞ p=∞.

Malliavin calculus

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {W}$$\end{document}W denote the the space of smooth and cylindrical random variables, i.e. random variables of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F =f(\xi (h_1),\dots , \xi (h_n))$$\end{document}F=f(ξ(h1),⋯,ξ(hn))for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1,\dots , h_n \in H$$\end{document}h1,⋯,hn∈H, and for some smooth f such that f and its partial derivatives of all orders have polynomial growth. The Malliavin derivative of such a random variable is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}_{\theta ,\zeta }F:= \sum _{i=1}^n \partial _{i}f(\xi (h_1),\dots , \xi (h_n)) h_i(\theta ,\zeta )$$\end{document}Dθ,ζF:=∑i=1n∂if(ξ(h1),⋯,ξ(hn))hi(θ,ζ)for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\theta ,\zeta ) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(θ,ζ)∈[0,1]×T where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _i$$\end{document}∂i denotes partial derivative with respect to the i-th argument. For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}k∈Z≥0, p \ge 1p≥1 the iterated Malliavin derivative \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {D}^k$$\end{document}Dk is closable as an operator from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p(\Omega )$$\end{document}Lp(Ω) into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_p(\Omega ; H^{\otimes k})$$\end{document}Lp(Ω;H⊗k). By convention, the 0-th Malliavin derivative is the identity map, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{\otimes 0} := \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}H⊗0:=R. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}_{\ge 0}$$\end{document}k∈Z≥0 and p \ge 1p≥1, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} completion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathscr {W}$$\end{document}W with respect to the norm\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F \mapsto \Vert F\Vert _{\mathscr {W}^k_p} :=\Big ( \mathbb The above definitions can be extended for the Hilbert-space valued case as follows. Proposition 4.2

(Malliavin integration by parts) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u, G_0 \in \mathscr {W}^n$$\end{document}u,G0∈Wn and let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f:\mathbb {R} \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}f:R→R be n times differentiable. Suppose moreover that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p \in [1,\infty )$$\end{document}p∈[1,∞), we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {E}\Vert \mathscr {D}u\Vert _{H}^{-p} <\infty .$$\end{document}E‖Du‖H-p<∞. Define iterated Skorokhod integrals recursively for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \{0,\dots , n-1\}$$\end{document}k∈{0,⋯,n-1} by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} G_{k+1} := \delta \Big (\frac{\mathscr {D}u}{\Vert \mathscr {D}u\Vert _H^2}G_k\Big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Gk+1:=δ(Du‖Du‖H2Gk).The following holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\big (\nabla ^n f(u) G_0\big ) = \mathbb {E}\big ( f(u) G_n\big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(∇nf(u)G0)=E(f(u)Gn).

We also recall the combinatorial notation from [10]. \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G} \subset \mathcal {F}$$\end{document}G⊂F and any random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z,\bar{Z} \in L_p(\Omega , C(\mathbb {T}))$$\end{document}Z,Z¯∈Lp(Ω,C(T)) and for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,1]×T we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\big \Vert F_{q,n}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})\big \Vert _{L^{\mathscr {G}}_{p_1,p_2}}\le N t^{n/4}\sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z(w)-\bar{Z}(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}.$$\end{document}‖Fq,n(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G≤Ntn/4supw∈T‖Z(w)-Z¯(w)‖Lp1,p2G.

Proof

We proceed by induction.

Step 1: We show that the statement holds for n=0n=0. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z,\bar{z} \in C(\mathbb {T})$$\end{document}z,z¯∈C(T), we have by (5.2) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi ^z(t,x) - \phi ^{\bar{z}}(t,x)&= P_t(z-\bar{z})(x) + \int _0^t\int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}(x,y)(\sigma (\phi ^z(r,y)) \\&\qquad - \sigma (\phi ^{\bar{z}}(r,y))\xi (dy,dr). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕz(t,x)-ϕz¯(t,x)=Pt(z-z¯)(x)+∫0t∫Tpt-r(x,y)(σ(ϕz(r,y))-σ(ϕz¯(r,y))ξ(dy,dr).Therefore, by the BDG inequality we get5.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert \phi ^z(t,x) - \phi ^{\bar{z}}(t,x)\Vert _{L_q}^2 \nonumber \\&\qquad \lesssim |P_{t}(z-\bar{z})(x)|^2 + \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}^2(x,y)\Vert \phi ^z(r,y) - \phi ^{\bar{z}}(r,y)\Vert _{L_q}^2 dy dr. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ϕz(t,x)-ϕz¯(t,x)‖Lq2≲|Pt(z-z¯)(x)|2+∫0t∫Tpt-r2(x,y)‖ϕz(r,y)-ϕz¯(r,y)‖Lq2dydr.In view of Proposition 5.2, we see that the norm in the integrand is bounded uniformly in (r, y). Hence using Lemma 11.4, we conclude that5.10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,z,\bar{z}) =\Vert \phi ^{z}(t,x) - \phi ^{\bar{z}}(t,x)\Vert _{L_q}&\lesssim \sup _{(t,w) \in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}}|P_{t}(z-\bar{z})(w)|\nonumber \\&\lesssim \sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}|z(w) - \bar{z}(w)|. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fq,0(2)(t,x,z,z¯)=‖ϕz(t,x)-ϕz¯(t,x)‖Lq≲sup(t,w)∈[0,1]×T|Pt(z-z¯)(w)|≲supw∈T|z(w)-z¯(w)|.By the triangle inequality, it follows that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^{(2)}_{q,0}(t,x,\cdot ,\cdot ) \in C((C(\mathbb {T}))^2, \mathbb {R}).$$\end{document}Fq,0(2)(t,x,·,·)∈C((C(T))2,R). This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})$$\end{document}Fq,0(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯) is indeed defined as a random variable. We begin by showing that the desired inequality holds for the case when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert Z\Vert _{\mathbb {B}},\Vert \bar{Z}\Vert _{\mathbb {B}} < N$$\end{document}‖Z‖B,‖Z¯‖B<N almost surely, for some constant N<∞ N<∞. By evaluating (5.9) at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(z,\bar{z}) = (Z,\bar{Z})$$\end{document}(z,z¯)=(Z,Z¯), and then taking the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}$$\end{document}Lp1,p2G-norm of the square root of both sides, we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}\\&\quad \lesssim \Vert P_{t}(Z-\bar{Z})(x)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}+ \Big \Vert \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}^2(x,y)|F_{q,0}^{(2)}(r,y,Z,\bar{Z})|^2 dydr\Big \Vert _{L_{\frac{p_1}{2},\frac{p_2}{2}}^{\mathscr {G}}}^{1/2}\\&\quad \lesssim \sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z(w)-\bar{Z}(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1, p_2}^\mathscr {G}} \\&\quad \quad + \Big (\int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}^2(x,y)\Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(r,y,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}^2 dydr\Big )^{1/2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Fq,0(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G≲‖Pt(Z-Z¯)(x)‖Lp1,p2G+‖∫0t∫Tpt-r2(x,y)|Fq,0(2)(r,y,Z,Z¯)|2dydr‖Lp12,p22G1/2≲supw∈T‖Z(w)-Z¯(w)‖Lp1,p2G+(∫0t∫Tpt-r2(x,y)‖Fq,0(2)(r,y,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G2dydr)1/2,where to obtain the last expression, we used Minkowski’s inequality. Hence we may conclude that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}\Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}^2&\lesssim \Big (\sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z(w)-\bar{Z}(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}\Big )^2\\&\qquad \qquad + \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {T}} p_{t-r}^2(x,y)\Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(r,y,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}^2 dydr. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Fq,0(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G2≲(supw∈T‖Z(w)-Z¯(w)‖Lp1,p2G)2+∫0t∫Tpt-r2(x,y)‖Fq,0(2)(r,y,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G2dydr.Note that by (5.10) and due to the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert Z\Vert _{\mathbb {B}},\Vert \bar{Z}\Vert _{\mathbb {B}}\le N $$\end{document}‖Z‖B,‖Z¯‖B≤N, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sup _{r,y}\Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(r,y,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}} \lesssim 2N $$\end{document}supr,y‖Fq,0(2)(r,y,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G≲2N. Hence by the above inequality and by Lemma 11.4 we get\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert F^{(2)}_{q,0}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}} \lesssim \sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z(w) - \bar{Z}(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}.$$\end{document}‖Fq,0(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯)‖Lp1,p2G≲supw∈T‖Z(w)-Z¯(w)‖Lp1,p2G.We now remove the assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert Z\Vert _{\mathbb {B}},\Vert \bar{Z}\Vert _{\mathbb {B}}<N$$\end{document}‖Z‖B,‖Z¯‖B<N. Indeed, we define the sequence of truncations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(Z_N,{\bar{Z}}_N) := ((N\wedge Z) \vee (-N), (N\wedge \bar{Z}) \vee (-N))$$\end{document}(ZN,Z¯N):=((N∧Z)∨(-N),(N∧Z¯)∨(-N)). Then using the continuity of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,0}^{(2)}$$\end{document}Fq,0(2) and Fatou’s lemma, the above inequality, and the fact that truncation is a Lipschitz operation, we get that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})\Vert _{L_{p,\infty }^{\mathscr {G}}}&\le \liminf _{N \rightarrow \infty } \Vert F_{q,0}^{(2)}(t,x,Z_N,\bar{Z}_N)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}\\&\lesssim \liminf _{N \rightarrow \infty } \sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z_N(w) - \bar{Z}_N(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}\\&\le \sup _{w \in \mathbb {T}}\Vert Z(w) - \bar{Z}(w)\Vert _{L_{p_1,p_2}^{\mathscr {G}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Fq,0(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯)‖Lp,∞G≤lim infN→∞‖Fq,0(2)(t,x,ZN,Z¯N)‖Lp1,p2G≲lim infN→∞supw∈T‖ZN(w)-Z¯N(w)‖Lp1,p2G≤supw∈T‖Z(w)-Z¯(w)‖Lp1,p2G.This finishes the proof of the case n=0n=0.

Step 2: Suppose that for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \mathbb {N}$$\end{document}n∈N the result holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,0}^{(2)},\dots , F_{q,n-1}^{(2)}$$\end{document}Fq,0(2),⋯,Fq,n-1(2) for all q\ge 2q≥2. We aim to show that it also holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,n}^{(2)}$$\end{document}Fq,n(2). We first assume that for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t,x)\in [0,1]\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}(t,x)∈[0,1]×T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,n}^{(2)}(t,x,z,\bar{z})$$\end{document}Fq,n(2)(t,x,z,z¯) is continuous with respect to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(z,\bar{z})$$\end{document}(z,z¯) so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_{q,n}^{(2)}(t,x,Z,\bar{Z})$$\end{document}Fq,n(2)(t,x,Z,Z¯) is a well-defined random variable. We will verify this continuity property later. By Proposition 5.3, we have5.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&F^{(2)}_{q,n}(t,x,z,\bar{z})\nonumber \\&\quad =\Vert \mathscr {D}^n[\phi ^z(t,x) -
